First, `hasActiveAdmin` matches the literal `'admin'` (`packages/db/src/repo/access.ts:63-70`), so a **super_admin-only organisation counts as adminless** and elevates the next manager it appoints. Second, `authorize.ts:105` is `matrixAllows || personHasActiveGrant`, so the grant alone passes the capability check — the elevated manager is indistinguishable from an admin at that layer. Found 2026-09-08 by lane D while specifying T-242, and **reproduced through the real entry point**: with T-242's rank check absent, a manager elevated this way (never hand-granted) successfully demoted an admin. Logged by the orchestrator on the lane's report | `partly fixed — T-244 (495cf07); lifecycle DECIDED by Ian 2026-09-09, building as T-248` | ⭐ **DECIDED, 2026-09-09: Ian chose Option 1 — revoke the elevated set when an organisation gains an active administrator.** Written up as **bible §13 row 188**, which is the authority; the conditional-grant alternative is **rejected and must not be re-proposed**, because it reintroduces at the grant layer the check-time fuzzy predicate row 115 removed at the role layer. He accepted the stated cost: a manager mid-shift loses the capability the instant the revocation commits. Building as **T-248**, no migration expected. ⛔ The trap that task must solve: a manager may hold `manage_users` by **hand-grant** (row 116's explicit-tick path) rather than auto-elevation, and revoking that would be a silent unrequested demotion — the two must be distinguishable at revocation time, or that is a BLOCKED finding. ⭐ **The inner bug is FIXED as T-244** (lane D): `hasActiveAdmin` (`access.ts:63-70`) now counts `super_admin` alongside `admin` (`role IN ('admin','super_admin')`), so a super_admin-only organisation is no longer read as adminless and stops elevating the next manager it appoints. Exactly **one** production caller (`roles.ts:97`), confirmed by a whole-tree grep and independently by the QA lane. The fix restores consistency rather than inventing it — `roles.ts:241`'s `isAdministrative()` is already literally `role === 'admin' || role === 'super_admin'`, asking the identical question one layer up; `hasActiveAdmin` was the outlier. ⭐ **Blast radius found while REPRODUCING, not reading:** `roles.test.ts`'s DEF-234 case 5 *depended* on this bug — a super_admin-only org was how it got a manager holding `manage_users`, the precondition T-242's rank ladder exists to refuse. After the fix that setup silently stops elevating, so the case would have **kept passing while testing nothing about rank**, refused by `authorize()` for holding no capability at all, with no red anywhere to notice by. Rebuilt to hand-grant via `grantRawHere` (case 4's technique); assertions unchanged, and the precondition is now *asserted* rather than inferred from the refusal. QA independently closed the search space: one caller means any affected test must call `assignRole`, so the 13 files that do were read individually — case 5 is the whole blast radius. 468 integration passed on the shared DB, 1169 unit, typecheck and build clean. No migration. ⛔ **STILL OPEN and it is Ian's**: the grant lifecycle — what happens to a manager already elevated when an admin later arrives. Written up in `docs/tasks/244-*.md` §6 with both shapes costed and a recommendation (**Option 1, revoke on admin-arrival**), because Option 2's conditional grants re-introduce the check-time fuzzy predicate **bible row 115 already rejected once**, one table down. Not built, deliberately. Original assessment retained: T-242 makes it **non-escalating** — the rank ladder refuses a rank-1 manager acting on a rank-2 admin whatever capabilities they hold — so this is a defect, not an emergency, and it should not be rushed. What remains is the grant lifecycle itself: an elevation justified by a *condition* outliving the condition. Two candidate shapes, and it is a real decision rather than an oversight to patch: revoke the elevated set when an organisation gains an active administrator, or mark those rows as conditional at grant time and have `authorize` ignore them once the condition lapses. ⚠️ Whichever is chosen, `hasActiveAdmin` not counting a `super_admin` is a separate bug inside this one and is the reason a well-run organisation can trip it |

| **DEF-241** | 🔸 | **`shareat-month-end.timer` is the only scheduled unit with no timezone, so it runs at 02:00 UTC — 10:00 Manila, mid-working-day.** `OnCalendar=*-*-01 02:00:00` with no zone; systemd reads that as UTC. Every sibling that cares about wall-clock time pins `Asia/Manila` **and says why in a comment**: finding-observation 01:30, recurring-expense-draft 02:00 (*"after finding-observation… so a draft created this morning is not counted as if it had existed all of yesterday"*), order-wastage-backstop 02:30 (*"after finding-observation and recurring-expense-draft, so this run does not contend"*). The other two units are cadence-only (`hourly`, `*:0/2`) and timezone-irrelevant. Month-end is the sole wall-clock unit that neither pins a zone nor explains the omission — and in a file family where every neighbour documents its slot, **the missing comment is the signal**. Confirmed live on the droplet 2026-09-09: `systemctl list-timers` shows its NEXT as `Thu 2026-10-01 02:00:00 UTC` while recurring-expense-draft shows `18:00 UTC` for its 02:00 Manila slot. ⚠️ **Not asserting a correctness bug** — the Manila month has fully ended by 10:00 Manila on the 1st, so the period boundary is the same either way. What is wrong is that a month-end close runs during business hours, outside the quiet window its siblings were deliberately sequenced into, and nothing records whether that was chosen. Found 2026-09-09 by the orchestrator while arming the T-216/T-178 timers (RULES §13 row 164 — log what you see, even when it is not the task)| **`fixed`** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-09-10 — Ian ruled it an OVERSIGHT, not a choice, and moved it to the small hours.** `OnCalendar=*-*-01 03:00:00 Asia/Manila` — 03:00 puts month-end LAST of the four wall-clock units, after order-wastage-backstop (02:30), recurring-expense-draft (02:00) and finding-observation (01:30), so it contends with none of them. ⭐ **The comment is half the fix**: the unit now states why 03:00, that the zone is load-bearing, what the old unzoned line did (02:00 UTC = 10:00 Manila), and that the zone must never be removed — because the absence of a comment was itself the tell that this was an oversight rather than a decision. ⚠️ **NOT YET LIVE — this is Ian’s to apply.** `docs/ops` does not ship (DEF-194), so the droplet still runs the old unzoned unit until the file is copied and `systemctl daemon-reload` + re-enable is run there. Snapshots for migrations 20–138 were never committed. Running `db:generate` today diffs the live schema against that 2019-era baseline and immediately raises a false rename-ambiguity prompt on an unrelated table (`expense_categories.is_system` vs a long-gone `sub_category` column) **before it reaches whatever table you were adding** — so it blocks ANY lane's next generated migration, not just the one that hit it. Found 2026-09-09 by lane E while adding `prerequisite_refusals` for T-245 (RULES §13 row 164 — logged even though off-task), and verified independently by the orchestrator: 13 snapshots, highest 0019, journal length 123. ⚠️ **Nothing is broken at runtime** — migrations 0020–0139 exist as SQL, are journalled, and apply correctly; several recent ones (0130, 0138, and now 0139) were evidently hand-authored the same way. What is broken is the *generator*, and the workaround has quietly become the convention without anyone recording it | `fixed 2026-09-10 — DOCUMENT, not repair; db:generate retired; ADR-012 ACCEPTED by Ian; bible row 191 APPLIED` | ⭐ **ANSWERED 2026-09-10 (Fable, on the orchestrator's brief) — WRITE THE CONVENTION DOWN; DO NOT REPAIR — and the reason is NOT the snapshot.** The feared failure was measured, not argued: a scratch database migrated from zero (124 applied, head 0140, 127 tables), the TS schema serialised through drizzle-kit's own `generateDrizzleJson`, and the two compared object by object from `pg_catalog`. **A regenerated snapshot would be TRUTHFUL: 0 real disagreements across 127 tables, 1,636 columns, 518 FKs, 312 indexes, 356 checks, 4 views** (3 flags, all notation — `bigserial` vs `bigint`+sequence, one `::numeric` cast). Caveat kept: 157 of 518 FKs carry a different NAME on disk (`_fkey` style) from drizzle's, so a generated ALTER on one would drop by the wrong name and fail loudly. ⛔ **What CANNOT be repaired is the journal half, and it is structural:** drizzle-kit 0.30.6 stamps `when: +new Date()` (verified in `bin.cjs`, `writeResult`), while this journal sits on DEF-091's one-day-per-index grid — **90 of 124 entries are in the future and the head (0140) is dated 2026-12-25** — so every generated entry sorts ~106 days BELOW the head and the migrator **silently skips it (DEF-039)**. No drizzle-kit option controls `when`; the head advances a day per migration, so the clock never catches up. A repaired baseline would therefore turn a tool that refuses into one that writes an unappliable journal entry on every use. Add that every table here needs what it cannot emit anyway (RLS on all 127 tables, 122 policies, 6 expression indexes, 2 EXCLUDEs, 45 triggers — TS declares none of them), and that the chain broke within 20 migrations while the tool still worked and stayed broken for 104 more. **Done:** `npm run db:generate` now runs `packages/db/generate-retired.ts` — three reasons, checklist pointer, writes nothing, exit 1 — guarded by `db-generate-retired.test.ts`; the convention lanes were already following is written down with its guards in **ADR-012 §5**; RULES rule 9 and CLAUDE.md's router point at it; DEF-073 and DEF-121 close to the same ADR. Proven from zero first and last on a scratch DB, which was then dropped; no shared database touched. ⚠️ Also found and recorded in the ADR: at this version pairing (kit 0.30.6, orm 0.38.4, PG 18) `drizzle-kit introspect` needs a module the orm does not ship and the API's `pushSchema` rejects PG18 expression indexes — nothing depends on either. ⛔ The read-only diff-aid alternative is recorded as *not adopted, and why* (ADR-012 §6b), with the condition for revisiting. ⭐ **RATIFIED AND REDISCOVERY-PROOFED THE SAME DAY, at Ian's instruction** (*"document this well … so it won't be rediscovered as it was before your work … on bible see where it fits"*): bible §13 **row 191** applied, plus §2 rule 8 and §15.5; ADR-012 is ACCEPTED. The knowledge now lives where the next person stands — the command's own output (`generate-retired.ts`), **`packages/db/migrations/README.md`** at the top of the folder they open to add 0141, the header of `migration-journal.test.ts` whose grid is the reason, RULES rule 9, CLAUDE.md's "Adding a table" row, and this row; DEF-073 and DEF-121 now say *closed* in their STATUS cells so a grep reads closed, not open. The incidental findings got rows of their own so they are not rediscovered either: **DEF-264** (the concurrent-commit sweep that happened during this task), **DEF-265** (drizzle-kit cannot read a PG18 database at this version pairing), **DEF-266** (119 database objects `schema/` does not declare, and 157 FK names). ORIGINAL: Two candidate directions, and the choice matters more than the effort: (a) regenerate and commit a current snapshot so `db:generate` works again — but a snapshot produced from today's schema may not represent the true state at idx 139 and could make a FUTURE diff subtly wrong; or (b) accept hand-authored SQL as the convention and **say so** in the bible plus a guard, since silently unusable tooling is how three lanes each waste an afternoon rediscovering this. ⛔ Not a lane's call: it decides how every future schema change in this project is written. ⚠️ Whichever is chosen, the trap to record is that the failure surfaces as an unrelated question about `expense_categories` — nothing points at the missing snapshots |

| **DEF-244** | 🔸 | ⭐ **The dense table rows on `/customers/[customerId]` are too short to host a real touch target, and that — not the CSS class — is the actual constraint.** Measured by lane B, 2026-09-09, compiling real CSS with the project's own installed Tailwind CLI (v3.4.19) against the page's actual markup at 390px: two consecutive "entry" links given a **44px** tap box (`min-h-11 -my-3.5`) **overlap by 3.2px** — row 1's box spans 47.6–91.6, row 2's 88.4–132.4 — and `document.elementFromPoint()` at the *visual gap* between the rows returns **row 2's link**, not neutral space. Confirmed for every consecutive pair. ⛔ **That is worse than the defect it fixes:** a small target you miss is a nuisance; a large one that opens a different customer's journal entry is a wrong action taken confidently. The other two contexts measured **safe** at 44px — `admin/stores/[id]`'s readiness panel keeps a 28px gap under `space-y-3`, and `settings/expense-categories`' Rename/Retire pair keeps 6.4px — so this is specific to that one table, not general to dense rows ⭐ **DECIDED BY IAN, 2026-09-10: the WHOLE ROW becomes the tap target** — not a taller row with a small link inside it. The standard mobile-list pattern (contacts, mail, banking): rows sit flush with no gap, so the overlap this defect is about becomes **impossible** rather than merely avoided — there is no inter-row space for a tap to fall into. It also lets rows stay compact, because a 44px target no longer has to fit INSIDE a shorter row; the row IS the target. **Reserved as T-258** (`docs/tasks/258-def-244-tappable-rows.md`), which supersedes T-249’s 32px compromise on this page. ⚠️ The spec must resolve rows that need a SECOND action before building, and must not widen into a sweep — this defect’s own measurement found the other two dense contexts safe at 44px | **`decided`** | **T-249 ships 32px (`py-2 -my-2`) uniformly**, ruled by the orchestrator: it is safe in the context that broke, and by monotonicity safe in the others, and it preserves the one-class property that stops the idiom being hand-typed a tenth time. ⚠️ **32px does NOT meet this product's touch bar and must not be read as the settled answer** — Apple is 44pt, Material 48dp, and T-241 deliberately chose 42px for buttons on Ian's touch-first rule. It is the best available *while holding zero visible layout change*. **The real fix is to give those rows height**, which is a layout change and a genuine design question for a product whose canvas is a phone portrait screen — not a CSS class question. Sequenced after T-249 so the shared class exists first, and grouped naturally with **DEF-243**'s five remaining instances |
| **DEF-245** | ⚠️ | ⭐ **The full integration suite cannot be run locally — two consecutive sessions have failed to complete it, by different failure modes.** 2026-09-08 the previous orchestrator had `npm run test:integration` **killed by the OS for memory pressure** (recorded then as "killed, not failed"). 2026-09-09 it **stalled**: ~40 minutes, a **12.8 MB log containing only Postgres truncate NOTICEs and not a single test-progress marker**, then the log size froze while 33 node processes stayed alive; the orchestrator stopped it. ⛔ **Neither run produced a pass or a fail — both produced an ABSENCE OF EVIDENCE**, which is the more dangerous outcome because a gate that cannot be run stops being a gate and quietly becomes a formality. The suite is serial by configuration (`fileParallelism: false` in `vitest.config.mts`) and 107+ files touch real Postgres, so wall-clock cost is expected — what is not expected is producing no progress output at all before freezing. ⚠️ Targeted runs are healthy throughout: 468 tests across two suites, 22, and 455, all on the shared database, all fast. So the problem is the *whole-suite* run, not the tests. Found 2026-09-09 by the orchestrator during the wrap-up ritual, which requires this gate | `reported` | Investigate before working around it a third time — the workaround (targeted runs plus CI) has silently become the convention across two sessions without anyone deciding it, which is the same shape as DEF-242. ⚠️ **Do not simply raise a timeout**: the log froze rather than ran slowly, so the first question is *what it was doing*, and the reporter emitting nothing for 40 minutes is itself a clue (a serial run with no per-file output cannot be distinguished from a hang). Candidate directions: run with a reporter that flushes per file, cap Node heap explicitly, or shard the suite. ⛔ Until then **CI (`ci.yml:68`) is the only real integration gate** and a wrap-up must say so rather than implying a local green. ⭐ **RAN TO COMPLETION 2026-09-09 (23:31–00:02) — `107 files, 2666 passed, 0 failed, exit 0, 1851s`** — the first finished local run in three sessions, on the shared `shareat_test` at head 0140, using this row's own suggested remedy: **`npx vitest run --project integration --reporter=verbose`**. ⛔ **State the conclusion narrowly, because the tempting one is bigger than the evidence.** This does NOT prove the default reporter causes a hang. What it proves is that **the suite is healthy and takes ~31 minutes**, and that under the default reporter it emits nothing for that entire time — so a 40-minute abort looks *identical* to a hang. This row's own words: *"a serial run with no per-file output cannot be distinguished from a hang."* ⚠️ **The most likely reading of 2026-09-09's stall is therefore that a working run was killed for looking dead**, and the 12.8 MB of truncate NOTICEs with no test markers is what a healthy serial run's stderr looks like when the reporter is silent. ⚠️ **The 2026-09-08 failure is NOT explained by this** — that one was OS-killed for memory pressure with lanes running concurrently, a different failure mode; my run had no peer lane doing heavy work. So the memory ceiling is untested and may still bite. ⭐ **And it bit within minutes of the run finishing, which is the corroboration this caveat needed:** two trivial `until … sleep 10` waiter loops were killed by the host **"because the system is running low on memory"** at 00:05, immediately after the 31-minute suite. Nothing was lost (the run had already completed and its numbers were read), but it shows the machine genuinely reaches memory pressure around a full integration run — so 2026-09-08's OS-kill was real and is **not** superseded by tonight's success. ⛔ **Do not read "it ran once" as "it runs reliably":** it ran once, on a quiet machine, with no peer lane doing heavy work. The reporter finding explains the *stall*; it does not explain, and does not fix, the *memory* failure. ⭐ **The cheap durable fix is to make `test:integration` verbose by default**, so nobody ever again has to distinguish a silent 31-minute run from a hang. ⚠️ **And a trap found on the way, worth more than the run:** the first attempt used `--reporter=basic`, which does not exist in this Vitest — it died loading the reporter, **the shell pipeline still exited 0**, and it read as a clean pass. Valid names: `default`, `verbose`, `dot`, `json`, `junit`, `tap`, `tap-flat`. **Check the command's own exit line, never the pipeline's** |

(2) **Sanitise the fallback** — parse `constraint "…"` out of the driver text and render a generic-but-presentable sentence, so an unmapped constraint degrades to plain language instead of SQL. Cheaper, covers all 390 at once, and never drifts; the price is a vaguer sentence where a specific one was possible. ⭐ They compose: (2) as the floor, (1) for the screens that deserve a specific sentence. ⚠️ Do not treat this as a prerequisite for DEF-230 — that row is about the *lookup*, this one is about the *coverage*. ⭐ **IAN DECIDED, 2026-09-09: option (b) now, then COUNT and improve by hit rate.** Sanitise the fallback first so nothing ever shows SQL again, then instrument it to record which constraints actually fire, and write specific sentences for the ones that hit most. ⭐ This is his own *count first* ruling (bible row 183 / T-245) applied a second time — and **T-245 already built the counter machinery, so reuse it rather than inventing a second one.** ⛔ **A counted hit is a DOUBLE signal, and the second half is worth more than the first.** Lane C captured a real unmapped failure through the real form at 390px and found the reason it reached the database at all: `createCategoryAction` (`apps/erp/src/app/settings/expense-categories/actions.ts:67`) casts the form's `group` field with a bare TypeScript `as` and passes it straight to the insert — **no runtime guard anywhere**, so the CHECK constraint is the only validation. Tested under genuine concurrency (two tabs, one session, `Promise.all`, no sequential await) on the one mapped constraint with no obvious TS equivalent for the race window: the TS pre-check won both times. ⛔ **That reframes "finish the map" as the low-value half** — the exposure is entirely in the ~390 unmapped, which is exactly where there is often no TS guard either |

| **DEF-249** | ⚠️ | ⭐ **THERE IS NO CODE PATH IN THE RUNNING APPLICATION THAT CAN GIVE A STORE ITS FIRST ATTENDANCE POLICY — so attendance at such a store is not degraded, it is INOPERATIVE, and an administrator who notices cannot fix it from the product.** `seedAttendanceConfig` has **13 call sites across 10 test files** and ZERO production callers. ⚠️ **The original filing said “twelve test callers” and “12 integration test files”; both numbers were wrong and neither was ever re-counted** — DEF-243’s shape a third time, found by the lane sent to trace the consequence rather than to audit the count. `packages/domain/src/attendance/config.ts:114` is invoked from 12 integration test files and from nothing else in the app — `createStore` (`packages/domain/src/stores/service.ts`) never calls it, and contains no reference to attendance at all. ⛔ **The comments assert the opposite:** `packages/db/schema/attendance-config.ts:117` says "every store is created fresh and `seedAttendanceConfig` inserts with no explicit…", and a sibling function's error text says "Every store gets one at creation." Neither is true. ⛔ **This is the exact shape that makes the suite useless as evidence:** every attendance test hand-seeds the config in its own fixture, so all of them run against a configured store while every real store has none — the tests cannot fail on the thing that is actually broken. Same family as DEF-226/DEF-227 (a function that exists, is tested, and is never called). ⚠️ **It also explains a symptom, which is how it was found:** all four stores in `shareat_test_t240` — including ones created through the ordinary path — have no attendance config, so `/settings/attendance` is a dead end offering only "supersede" and no way to create a first row. Found 2026-09-09 by lane C when a screen it needed for an unrelated capture could not be reached; it correctly reported the dead end instead of forcing past it, and the dead end turned out to be the defect ⭐ **TRACED AND ANSWERED 2026-09-10** (lane C, Opus, read-only — `docs/discovery/31-def-249-attendance-config-consequence.md`): **THIS IS A WIRING BUG, NOT A LIVE PAYROLL PROBLEM. No money has been computed wrong and none can be** — a store with no config cannot produce a single `attendance_days` row, so the arithmetic that could be wrong never runs. **Proven as a single-writer chain, not a judgement:** `upsertDay` has exactly ONE caller in the tree (`adjudicate.ts:158`), and `adjudicateDay` loads the config at `:126` and THROWS before `computeDay` at `:146`; `recordPunch` is the same shape, its one production caller (`sync/ingestor.ts:334`) sitting downstream of a quarantine that returns ~15 lines earlier. **Verified independently by the orchestrator rather than accepted:** `upsertDay` → 1 caller, `insertConfig` → 2 callers (both in `config.ts`), `seedAttendanceConfig` → 0 non-test files. ⛔ **THE NEAR-MISS IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING: there is NO default config anywhere**, by design (`schema/attendance-config.ts:27-33`, *“unset cannot exist”*). Had a read-time default merge existed, an unconfigured store would have been handed a complete FICTIONAL policy — 9h shifts, `shortShiftHalfDayOt` true, OT ×1.25, night differential on — and DEF-249 WOULD have been a live payroll problem. **The refusal is the only thing standing between this defect and miscomputed pay.** ⚠️ One ordering accident noted rather than relied on: the `catch` at `ingestor.ts:361-378` COULD have swallowed the throw, and is unreachable at an unconfigured store only because the quarantine fires first. ⛔ **NOT PROVEN, and it matters: production was NOT queried.** The no-miscomputation verdict comes from the code and holds on any database, but *“no production store has an attendance config”* has never been counted — one command, needs production access. ⭐ **Correlation with a mechanism in `shareat_dev`:** the two stores created 2026-08-31 hold all five config rows (all stamped within 15ms, first of each with `created_by` NULL — one bulk insert, not the app) plus all 13 attendance days, 4 punches and the only payslip; the three stores created afterwards through the ordinary path have 0 config, 0 days, 0 punches. | `reported` | ⚠️ **Verify the production consequence before choosing a fix, because it is not yet known.** `getLiveConfig`'s own error text warns that "an unconfigured attendance policy fails quietly in the direction that looks fine" — so the first question is what payroll and adjudication actually DO against an unconfigured store today, which nobody has traced. That answer decides whether this is a wiring bug or a live payroll risk. ⛔ **`createStore` ALONE IS INSUFFICIENT, and the trace proves it — it fixes FUTURE stores only.** 53 QA stores, 4 in `t240` and 3 of 5 dev stores already exist with no route to a first policy, and `/settings/attendance` offers only *supersede*, which needs a live row to supersede — so the screen renders “no attendance config yet” with no form, and the action behind it could not have worked either. **The “first policy” capability needs a REACHABLE SURFACE in the product, not merely a call added to store creation.** ⭐ **Then fix the wiring at `createStore`, not at the call sites** — and add the caller-existence guard — ⛔ **but NOT as this register originally described it. A guard that counts CALLERS sees THIRTEEN here and is satisfied. It must count PRODUCTION callers, i.e. callers outside `tests/`** — ⛔ **and even that is NOT a lint rule; see the census (`docs/discovery/32-production-caller-census.md`). A mechanical “every export needs a non-test caller” fires **111 times on day one and is wrong about ~108**, so it earns an `eslint-disable` the same afternoon and the fifth DEF-249 then ships *under a suppression comment*, which is strictly worse than no guard. **Build a RATCHET instead:** freeze today's census as a guard-REGENERATED baseline and fail only when a NEW export joins the set. ⚠️ The baseline must never be hand-edited (DEF-240's lesson — the hand-maintained half is the half that rots), and it must strip comments/strings before matching, detect imports from raw text, and count `scripts/` and the scheduler as PRODUCTION. ⭐ Cheaper still and it catches more: **re-run the census at each phase boundary and diff it** — one command, no suppression mechanism, and it would have caught DEF-226, DEF-227 and DEF-249 before any of them cost a lane. ⭐ **The NUMBER is the artifact here, not the recommendation.** The do-not-build-the-lint-rule conclusion rests on 111 firings and ~108 of them wrong; if a later census shows that ratio has moved substantially, **re-derive the conclusion rather than inherit it**. ⚠️ And the scanner itself must ship with its control built in and failing CLOSED — both of the census's own runs produced a complete, plausible, WRONG list rather than an error, and neither was visible in the output — the directory-scope complaint (guards only checking `packages/db/src/repo/*`) was the smaller half of why this hid. ⛔ Do NOT backfill blindly — **though the trace makes this SOFTER than it feared, not harder: since no unconfigured store has any attendance day at all, a chosen `effective_from` cannot retroactively restate anything anyone was paid. It needs to be HONEST, not safe.** An attendance policy still has an `effective_from` and choosing it is a data decision, not a migration — still Ian’s |
| **DEF-250** | ⚠️ | ⭐ **The nightly jobs and first-run setup depend on the database connection being a bypass role, and nothing says so — so the day anyone tightens the production role, every scheduled job silently processes ZERO organisations and reports success.** `listOrgsToRun` must see every organisation, which no single tenant principal can express; `bootstrapOrganization` cannot scope to an organisation that does not exist yet. Both survive today only because `DATABASE_URL` happens to connect as a role that bypasses RLS — an implicit dependency, taken rather than declared. ✅ **ANSWERED 2026-09-11 — MEASURED ON PRODUCTION BY IAN, AT THE ORCHESTRATOR’S REQUEST, THROUGH THE APP’S OWN `DATABASE_URL` ON THE PRODUCTION DROPLET.** `current_user = doadmin`, `rolsuper = f`, **`rolbypassrls = t`**. And on `expenses`, `journal_entries`, `stores`: `relrowsecurity = t`, `relforcerowsecurity = t`, `owner = doadmin`. ⚠️ **The database is NOT on the droplet** — it is a DigitalOcean **managed** cluster reached on the private endpoint at port 25060, which is new information and is why `psql` was not even installed on the host. ⛔ **PROVEN, NOT CITED, because last night an RLS probe on this exact question returned a confident wrong verdict by measuring the wrong layer:** the orchestrator built a role with `BYPASSRLS` that OWNS a table with RLS `ENABLE`d, `FORCE`d, and a `USING (false)` deny-all policy — and it **saw the row**. So `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` does NOT constrain a `BYPASSRLS` role; the role attribute wins over both the policy and FORCE. ⭐ **Therefore RLS contributes NOTHING on the production connection. The 122 policies are sound and inert.** ⚠️ **This is still not a breach and the ⚠️ severity stands:** `authorize()` runs on every path, the repository layer still filters by org, and there is one organisation on production today. What is now measured rather than assumed is that bible §15.11 — *no layer may be the only one for a given risk* — is **not satisfied for tenancy in production**: the backstop everyone believed was underneath is switched off for the role the app connects as. ⭐ **So this row is no longer a hypothesis about production — the bypass it depends on is CONFIRMED present, which means the failure it describes is not hypothetical either: it is what WILL happen the moment anyone tightens that role, and tightening it is now a thing we actively want to do (DEF-282). These paths must move onto `systemTransaction()` BEFORE the role is tightened, not after.** ⛔ **The failure direction is the dangerous one:** a tightened role does not error, it returns an empty organisation list, so the job runs, finds nothing to do, and exits 0. That is indistinguishable from a genuinely quiet night, which is the exact signal DEF-240's work made trustworthy. ⚠️ **It is reachable without anyone intending it** — hardening the production role is an ordinary thing to do for ordinary reasons, and nothing in the code or the docs warns that these paths depend on not doing it. Found 2026-09-09 by lane E while specifying T-251 (DEF-218), which is how it surfaced: the obvious fix for DEF-218 is to stop the *test* connection bypassing RLS, and the lane found that doing so naively would cause this failure in the suite first and production later. ⚠️ Confirmed present on the real request path, not only in jobs: `apps/erp/src/app/setup/actions.ts` opens a bare `transaction()` around `bootstrapOrganization` — verified by the orchestrator, not taken from the report. ⭐ The lane also counted **7 hand-rolled copies** of the org-listing idiom, so the dependency is duplicated, not centralised | `reported` | **T-251a is the fix and is specced** — give the system principal a name (`systemTransaction()` in `packages/db/src/client.ts`) and route the ~6 cross-org call sites through it. ⭐ **On today's role it changes no behaviour at all**; what it buys is that the set of places needing system authority becomes enumerable and greppable instead of being wherever someone happened to write a bare `transaction()`, which is why it ships BEFORE the role change rather than with it. ⚠️ It touches **bible §13 row 185**, which sanctions exactly one owner-role request path and says in terms that it is *not a precedent* — the lane's recommendation is that row 185's substance is unchanged and only its implementation pointer moves, so a future reader greps one doc comment instead of re-deriving the answer from `/setup`'s source. ⛔ **That amendment is Ian's to approve, not the orchestrator's to assume** |

This is RULES §3.4 / bible row 94 — *parallel agents must share no STATE, not merely no files* — with the register as the shared state, and neither session did anything its brief did not say. ⚠️ *"Has finished"* in a brief is a claim about another process, not a fact the reader can act on | `reported` | Three cheap rules, none new: (1) a brief that hands a tree over says how the reader can **verify** it is alone — `git log -3` with times, `ListAgents` — instead of asserting it; (2) a session that stages a shared document reads `git diff --cached -- <path>` for hunks it did not write before committing, because a foreign hunk means another session is live on the tree; (3) an agent that must not be swept works in a worktree (`EnterWorktree` / `superpowers:using-git-worktrees`), which is the tool that exists for exactly this. ⛔ Do not answer this with a `git stash` convention — a stash is one more piece of shared state |

0)`), never against a literal `0` — found only by reading the function fully and cross-checking `journal_lines_exactly_one_side_check`. That is the strictly-quieter class arriving where the quieter layer is also absent, which is worth knowing before DEF-277 is ranked. ⭐ **AND IT QUALIFIES DEF-272'S OWN SEVERITY CLAIM, which is why it is filed rather than mentioned:** DEF-272 says the defect is "reachable today on cash requests", and on the cash-request CREATE form it is not — `min="0.01"` makes the browser refuse `0.004` before the form is ever submitted, with its own message and no driver text. Built as T-270. DEF-241 moved the timer to `OnCalendar=*-*-01 03:00:00 Asia/Manila` to stop it running mid-working-day — correct in intent, but 03:00 Manila on the 1st **is 19:00 UTC on the last day of the previous month**, so `getUTCMonth()` still reads the outgoing month and the function returns one month too early. ⭐ **Executed, not reasoned:** closing February 2027 fires at `2027-02-28T19:00Z`, and `lastCompletedPeriod` returns **`2027-01`**. And nobody hit it in the app because `expenses/new` makes you hand-type a `cash_request` id into a free-text box, which realistically nobody has. ⛔ **THE SECOND-ORDER COST IS WORSE THAN THE ERROR, AND IT MAKES THE OBVIOUS FIX THE DANGEROUS ONE.** Reading *"account 1120 requires a party"* and dropping `requires_party` makes the error vanish and the expense save — and makes `floatPositionOf`'s `spent` bucket (which selects on `party_type = 'cash_request'`, `repo/cash-requests.ts:137-167`) never see the spend. `outstanding` never falls, the request never reaches `liquidated` (a state `schema/cash-requests.ts:38-40` says is *"reached by ARITHMETIC"*), and DEF-159's gate (`cash-requests/index.ts:199-209`) then refuses that person **every future float, permanently** — recoverable only by editing the database by hand. **A one-line fix that silently retires a staff member from ever holding company cash again.** Found 2026-09-11 by webapps-65 while specifying T-266; traced at source, then **proven by execution** | **`fixed`** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-09-11, T-266 (`docs/tasks/266-money-lines.md` §4).** `fundingLine` now parties `1120` with `partyType: 'cash_request'`, `partyId: cashRequestId` — **mirroring `releaseCashRequest` (`cash-requests/index.ts:241-249`), which debits the same account with exactly that party when the float goes out.** Released and spent had to name the same thing or the float could never close. A staff account with no `cashRequestId` now **blocks by name** (*"this is paid from a staff float, which belongs to a specific person — name the cash request it draws down, or the float can never be liquidated"*) rather than reaching the trigger, the same discipline the `credit`-without-supplier branch already applied. ⛔ **The misleading comment was corrected in the same edit** — leaving it would re-plant the assumption that hid the defect. ⭐ **WATCHED FAILING FIRST, and the red is the trigger's own words:** with the fix disabled, `packages/domain/tests/integration/expenses-staff-float.test.ts` gives `PostgresError: account 1120 (…) requires a party on every line, but none was given` on 4 of 5 cases — the 5th (office safe, no party) correctly still passing, which is what proves the fix is targeted rather than blanket. Closing tests: that integration file (5 cases, real Postgres — posts with the party · float arithmetic moves, `spent` 0→1500 and `outstanding` 2000→500 · blocks by name and leaves the draft intact · **two different floats on the SAME account stay separate**, the case `1120` exists for · office safe still party-less) and 6 new unit cases in `expense-routing.test.ts` asserting the party on every branch. ⚠️ The pre-existing *"routes to 1120"* unit test was left in place and annotated rather than rewritten — it is the evidence for why a test can cover a line and miss the defect |

| **DEF-293** | ⚠️ | ⛔ **ADR-006 REQUIRES A CLOSE GATE THAT CAN NEVER FIRE, AND A GATE THAT CANNOT FIRE IS WORSE THAN NO GATE.** Its Gates table requires *“no draft entries dated in the period”* and names no table. The literal reading — `journal_entries.status = 'draft'` — **cannot ever match a committed row**: draft is a within-transaction construction state. `repo/journal.ts:184` inserts the header as draft, `postDraftEntry` (`:255`) flips it to posted, both inside one transaction, with the deferred triggers firing at COMMIT. ⭐ **The danger is not the missing check, it is the FALSE ASSURANCE:** such a gate appears on the close checklist, passes on every period forever, and tells the person closing the month that something was verified. That is DEF-210’s shape — a layer that looks like a control and is not one. ✅ **T-264 slice 2 built it against the source documents that DO hold a persistent draft**, which are also the two ADR-006 names where it gets concrete: `expenses` (`business_date`) and `deliveries` (`delivered_on`). ⚠️ `sales_days` was deliberately excluded — its statuses are `open`, `submitted`, `verified` and `voided`, and `open` means the day is still happening, not that work was left unfinished; gating on it would refuse a close whenever a store was mid-trading. Found 2026-09-11 by lane F implementing the gate — the first person to try, which is why nobody had noticed | `fixed 2026-09-12` | ⭐ **Amend ADR-006’s Gates table to name the tables** — the implementation is already correct and shipped; what is stale is the ADR text, and a ratified document that says something the code deliberately does not do will mislead the next reader. ⚠️ **Ian’s to weigh, not a lane’s** — amending a ratified ADR is his call, and the alternative (leave the ADR and rely on the code comment) is defensible if he prefers the ADR frozen as a record of what was decided ✅ **RESOLVED 2026-09-12 — Ian ruled: amend the ADR.** `docs/decisions/ADR-006-period-close.md` §Gates now names `expenses` (`business_date`) and `deliveries` (`delivered_on`), records the deliberate `sales_days` exclusion, and carries an AMENDED note explaining why the literal `journal_entries.status = 'draft'` reading can never fire. ⚠️ **No code change — the implementation was already correct**; what was stale was the ratified text. |
| **DEF-294** | 🔸 | ⚠️ **THREE OF ADR-006’S FIVE CLOSE GATES ARE NOT PERIOD-SCOPED, SO `closeReadiness` ANSWERS A DIFFERENT QUESTION FROM THE ONE THE TABLE IMPLIES.** Trial-balance ties, subledger-sums-to-control and reciprocal drift are continuous, org-wide, all-time invariants reused from `listFindings` — correctly, and `closeReadiness` (`findings/index.ts:212`) is right to reuse them. ⭐ **But that means it answers “is the ledger currently sound”, not “is THIS period closeable”**, while ADR-006’s table reads as if all five gates were period-specific. ⚠️ **Not a defect in the code and not obviously a defect in the design** — a continuous invariant arguably SHOULD block a close, since you cannot honestly close a month on a ledger that does not balance. | **DEF-296** | 🔸 | ⚠️ **The pre-auth exemption list's “stale entry” guard cannot detect the staleness it warns about.** `tests/unit/no-direct-transaction-in-erp.test.ts`'s `PRE_AUTH_PATHS` comment says an exemption that no longer applies *“makes the rule look narrower than it is, and the next person widens it by adding a neighbour”*, and the assertion below it is titled *“every pre-auth exemption still matches a real file — a stale entry cannot grant cover”*. But the assertion only checks `files.includes(path)` — i.e. that the exemption names a file that still **exists**. It never checks that the file still **reaches a raw handle**. An exemption therefore survives intact after the file stops needing it, which is the far more likely kind of staleness: files get fixed much more often than they get deleted. ⭐ Demonstrated by T-267: converting `apps/erp/src/app/setup/lib.ts` and `apps/erp/src/app/sign-in/lib.ts` from `transaction()` to `systemTransaction()` removes their only raw-handle use, and the guard stays green with both exemptions still standing. ⚠️ The orchestrator predicted this guard would go RED on those two and it did not — lane A measured 1 failed unit file where the spec said 2, and was right. Same family as **DEF-295**: a guard whose check is narrower than the claim in its own title. ⭐ **FIXED 2026-09-12, and the fix found THREE dead entries, not the two T-267 created.** `apps/erp/src/app/setup/actions.ts` had carried a raw-handle exemption with no raw handle since it was converted to `systemTransaction()` — stale long before today and invisible for exactly as long. ⚠️ The orchestrator's own pre-check with `grep` said that file still reached a handle; it was counting matches inside COMMENTS. The guard's own comment-stripping `reachesRawHandle()` was right and the hand-rolled grep was wrong — use the guard's function, not a fresh regex | `fixed` | ⭐ Add a second assertion: for every `PRE_AUTH_PATHS` entry, `reachesRawHandle()` must be non-empty — an exemption for a file that no longer reaches one is removed, not kept. The function already exists in the file, so this is an assertion, not new machinery. ⛔ Land it TOGETHER WITH removing `setup/lib.ts` and `sign-in/lib.ts` from the list, or it fails on arrival. Orchestrator-owned: guards are a shared row, never a lane's to edit |

| **DEF-297** | ⚠️ | ⛔ **`deleteFile` CANNOT TELL WHETHER IT DELETED ANYTHING, and the orphan sweep counts on it having.** `packages/db/src/repo/files.ts:101-103` is `await tx.delete(storedFiles).where(and(eq(id, id), isNull(linkedTo)))` with **no `.returning()`**, typed `Promise<void>`. A DELETE matching zero rows is not an error in Postgres, so `sweepOrphanedFiles` increments `removed` and reports success for a row that is still there. ⭐ This is bible-row-194 era's *“silent write”* — the same shape as DEF-259's sign-out, which also `UPDATE … WHERE`'d nothing and said nothing. ⚠️ **The house already solves this and this function did not adopt it:** `packages/db/src/repo/fixed-assets.ts:311-322` (`updateTerms`) does `.returning({ id }) → length === 0 → throw`, and its own comment says *“changed nothing is indistinguishable from success”*. | **DEF-298** | ⛔ | ⛔⛔ **THE ORPHAN SWEEP DELETES STORAGE BYTES BEFORE CONFIRMING THE ROW IS STILL UNCLAIMED — A FILE ATTACHED SECONDS EARLIER LOSES ITS BYTES AND KEEPS ITS ROW.** `sweepOrphanedFiles` (`packages/domain/src/files/index.ts:204-215`) reads orphan candidates in one transaction, then calls `driver.remove(o.storagePath)` **unconditionally** for every candidate, and only afterwards calls `deleteFile`, whose `WHERE` correctly excludes a row that has since been linked. ⚠️ **The guard that protects the row is exactly what strands it.** If `linkFile` claims the file in the window between the read and the remove, the object store loses the bytes while the database row survives, pointing at a `storage_path` that no longer exists. For a receipt attached to an expense moments earlier that is **loss of the evidence for a journal entry**, not a miscount — and `deleteFile`'s own doc comment leans on `expenses.receipt_file_id` being `ON DELETE restrict`, which protects the ROW and does nothing whatever for the BYTES. ⚠️ Wider than DEF-287, which recorded this sweep only as a counting problem. Found by lane A (`webapps-4e`) while answering T-267 invariant 4; verified independently by the orchestrator from the source. ⭐ **SEVERITY RE-READ after DEF-299:** this has never fired in production, because the sweep has never been wired to anything (DEF-299). ⚠️ That makes it a **landmine rather than an active bleed**, and arguably more dangerous for it — whoever eventually schedules this cron would do so believing it had been running safely for months, and it would begin deleting bytes out from under just-claimed files on its first night | `fixed 2026-09-12 (T-269)` | Re-check `linked_to` immediately before `driver.remove`, or collapse read→remove→delete so the row is deleted first and the bytes removed only after that DELETE reports a row. ⛔ Delete-then-remove is the correct order: a deleted row with surviving bytes is a garbage-collection problem, a surviving row with deleted bytes is data loss. ⚠️ Needs `packages/db/src/repo/files.ts` and a restructure of the sweep — deliberately NOT folded into T-267, whose scope was four routing lines |

| **DEF-301** | ⚠️ | ⛔ **`CONSTRAINT_MESSAGES` IS MATCHED BY SEARCHING THE RAW ERROR TEXT, SO A HAND-WRITTEN `RAISE EXCEPTION` CAN NEVER BE GIVEN A FRIENDLY SENTENCE BY ADDING A KEY — AND THE ATTEMPT FAILS SILENTLY.** `errorMessage` (`apps/erp/src/lib/constraint-messages.ts`) maps a constraint by `for (const [constraint, message] of Object.entries(CONSTRAINT_MESSAGES)) if (raw.includes(constraint))` — a substring search over the driver's message, not a lookup on the parsed or structured constraint name. ⚠️ The five `CREATE CONSTRAINT TRIGGER` invariants of **DEF-279** never emit their own name in that text — that is the entirety of DEF-279 — so adding `journal_entries_balance_check: '…'` to the map would sit there matching nothing, for ever, with no error and no test failing. ⭐ **Found the hard way during T-270**, when the obvious remedy for a regression was *“just add proper entries for the five”* and reading the code showed that remedy is closed. Both of the lane's offered remedies were dead ends and the second was invisibly so. ⚠️ It also means the map's keys and the counter's names are **two different namespaces that look like one**: `recordIfUnmappedConstraint` now resolves a real constraint name from `err.constraint_name` (T-270), while `errorMessage` resolves nothing of the sort — it pattern-matches prose. A future reader will reasonably assume a name that the counter reports can be mapped, and it cannot | `fixed 2026-09-12` | Make the map a lookup on the RESOLVED constraint name (`resolveConstraintName(err)`, which T-270 added). ⛔ **SUPERSEDED 2026-09-12 — “falling back to the current substring scan for anything the resolver cannot name” was ALSO on this line and is now overruled (orchestrator, ruling to lane B on T-271).** That fallback would apply precisely where the resolver found no structural name — the population most likely to match a key by COINCIDENCE rather than by identity — so it would have preserved this defect's failure mode in the one place it is most dangerous and least visible, while letting everyone believe it was fixed. ⭐ **The asymmetry that settles it:** falling through to `genericConstraintMessage` or the prefix-stripped raw text is a QUIET, CORRECT failure (a less friendly sentence); matching the wrong friendly sentence by substring coincidence is a CONFIDENT, WRONG one (a precise explanation of a rule the person did not break). Prefer the quiet failure. ⚠️ **A disposition in this register is a hypothesis — the second time in one week.** DEF-279's was wrong the same way. This one was caught by lane B reading the row against the spec instead of taking either on trust. That unifies the two namespaces and makes an added key actually fire. ⛔ **Do NOT do this as part of a defect fix that happens to be nearby** — it changes which sentence every mapped constraint renders, so it needs its own task and its own before/after pass over the existing map. ⚠️ Until then, the five keep rendering their own hand-authored text, which is precise and actionable (*“journal entry X is unbalanced: debits 100.00 <> credits 90.00”*) and is **better** than the generic fallback — T-270 deliberately reverted to it ✅ **FIXED 2026-09-12 — T-271, `627a856`.** `errorMessage` resolves the name once via `resolveConstraintName` and looks it up exactly; no substring scan remains. All 26 mapped constraints provoked for REAL against Postgres render byte-identically before and after. ⛔ **AND THE REVIEW CAUGHT A REGRESSION THE 26-ROW TABLE STRUCTURALLY COULD NOT SEE.** The first pass gated the generic sanitiser on `resolveConstraintName` instead of `parseConstraintName`. Every one of the 26 is a native violation populating BOTH the structured field and the prose, so all 26 stayed green and the table was honestly complete — while DEF-279's five, which populate only the first, stopped falling through to their own text: *“journal entry 9f3a is unbalanced: debits 100.00 <> credits 90.00”* became *“That change isn't allowed for that record.”* ⚠️ **That is the exact regression T-270 deliberately reverted, made again — twice in one week by two different sessions, via two different routes.** The rule it violates: **the LOOKUP and the SANITISER answer different questions and need different resolvers.** *Which constraint is this?* → the structured field. *Is this raw Postgres grammar a person must never read?* → the name appearing in the PROSE. A hand-written `RAISE EXCEPTION` is not raw grammar; sanitising it destroys information to replace it with nothing. ⭐ **A unit-level guard over all five is T-271 part 2**, because review caught this twice and review is not a control you can rely on twice. |
| **DEF-302** | ⚠️ | ⛔ **THE FISCAL-PERIOD OVERRIDE IS UNREACHABLE ON THREE REAL FLOWS, AND IT FAILS THERE IN THE ONE WAY THAT CANNOT BE DIAGNOSED FROM THE ERROR.** T-264 slice 3 exempts the two journal triggers (`0003:214`, `0003:246`) from the period lock for `admin`/`super_admin`. It deliberately does NOT exempt `stock_movements_check_period_open` (`0063:111`) — a stock movement is not a journal entry, and back-dating one into a closed month recomputes inventory layers and therefore COGS, which is a decision about inventory that nobody has been asked. ⚠️ **But three production paths insert a stock movement and post a ledger entry on the SAME `tx`, movement FIRST:** `commissary/transfer.ts` `confirmReceipt` (`:385` → `:503`), `production/index.ts` `runProductionPosting` (`:444`/`:503` → `:547`), and `trade/returns.ts` `recordInternalReturn` (`:447` → `:485`) — all three posting the movement at the same date as the entry. ⛔ **So on those three, an authorised override never reaches `postEntry` at all:** the transaction rolls back at the movement insert, and the admin sees a STOCK error rather than a period error, with nothing anywhere indicating that the override was never consulted. A correctly-working system that reads as a broken one. ⭐ **Found by lane A answering a question the briefing asked rather than by any test** — *“does any path that calls `postEntry` also insert into `stock_movements` in the same transaction?”* The orchestrator had scoped `stock_movements` out expecting the answer to be zero paths; it is three. That file is in the Firebase console and cannot be read from here. ⭐ **One genuine mitigating factor, stated so the severity is not overstated:** object paths are `orgs/<orgId>/[stores/<storeId>/]<purpose>/<uuid>.<ext>` (`files/index.ts:119-122`) and the filename is a v4 UUID, so a reader must know the exact path — unless the rules also permit LIST, which `allow read` does cover in Firebase Storage, and which would make the whole tree walkable ⭐ **IAN ANSWERED, 2026-09-12: the cross-app fear is WRONG and is withdrawn.** His words: *“shareat-hub-v2 storage is sole for v2 this is the link gs://shareat-hub-v2”* — the storage is dedicated to ERPv2 and is not shared with POS or CUSTOMER, so the scenario this row was filed about (accounting receipts sitting beside the customer app's menu photos in one world-readable default bucket) does not exist. ⚠️ **The inference that produced it was reasonable and still wrong:** `CROSS-APP-MAP.md:57-58` records `shareat-hub-v2` as the Firebase PROJECT shared by POS and CUSTOMER, and the orchestrator read a shared project as implying a shared bucket. A project can host a bucket used by exactly one app. ⛔ **ONE NARROWER DISCREPANCY SURVIVES AND IS NOT CLOSED:** Ian names the bucket `gs://shareat-hub-v2`, production's running app logs `shareat-hub-v2.firebasestorage.app`, and this repo's own dev environment is configured with `shareat-hub-v2`. `firebaseConfigFromEnv` uses `FIREBASE_STORAGE_BUCKET` **verbatim and appends no suffix** (`packages/storage-firebase/src/index.ts:113`), so that log line is the literal value on the host — which is a different string from both the name Ian gave and the one dev uses. Either the two names denote one bucket loosely written, or production writes to a different bucket than the one intended. ⭐ **Harmless today either way** — production holds zero organisations, so nothing has been uploaded — and cheap to settle | `reported` — ⚠️ **the cross-app concern is WITHDRAWN by Ian's ruling; what remains open is only which bucket production actually writes to** | ⛔ **CHECK THIS BEFORE THE FIRST REAL RECEIPT IS UPLOADED, NOT AFTER — it is minutes of work and the downside is disclosed supplier pricing going back years.** Two questions settle it, both in the Firebase console: (1) is `shareat-hub-v2` a distinct bucket created for ERPv2, or the project's default bucket? (2) what are that bucket's storage rules — `allow read;` or deny-all? ⭐ **If (b), the fix is not a code change**: create a dedicated private bucket, set it deny-all, point `FIREBASE_STORAGE_BUCKET` at it, and move any objects already written. ⚠️ **Production currently holds zero organisations, so in all likelihood nothing has been uploaded yet — which is exactly why this is cheap today.** ⛔ **Do NOT assume (a) because the name looks deliberate.** `.env.example` predicted this exact confusion and wrote a warning against it, which means somebody already thought the two values were easy to conflate |
| **DEF-312** | 🔸 | **T-272 MADE `stored_files_unlinked_idx` STALE: THE ORPHAN SWEEP NOW FILTERS ON A COLUMN THE INDEX DOES NOT CARRY.** `stored_files_unlinked_idx` is `ON ("uploaded_at") WHERE "linked_to" is null` (`0032:50`, mirrored at `schema/stored-files.ts:85`) — a partial index built for the sweep's ORIGINAL query, which had no organisation predicate because the sweep was global by design (DEF-287). ⭐ **T-272 scoped the sweep per organisation**, so `listOrphans` now asks `org_id = $1 AND linked_to IS NULL AND uploaded_at < now() - interval`, and the index can serve the partial condition and the ordering but not the `org_id` equality — Postgres fetches candidates across every organisation and discards the ones that are not the caller's. ⚠️ **Harmless at today's scale and worth saying so:** production holds zero organisations, the query caps at 500 rows (`ORPHAN_SWEEP_BATCH_LIMIT`), and the partial index is already narrow because most rows have `linked_to` set. This is a correctness-neutral efficiency drift, not a bug. ⭐ **The reason it is worth a row at all is the SHAPE:** an index is a recorded assumption about a query, and T-272 changed the query without the assumption going anywhere — nothing failed, nothing slowed measurably, and no gate could have noticed. That is the same family as this week's guards whose scope stopped matching their claim, expressed in the schema rather than in a test. ⭐ Noticed in passing by lane A while rewriting `listOrphans` (RULES §13 row 164); verified against both the migration and the schema file before filing | `verified` | ⭐ **Do not fix it on its own — it is not worth a migration by itself**, and this project does not edit applied migrations (RULES §1 rule 9), so it would need a new one to drop and recreate the index. **Fold it into the next migration that touches `stored_files` for another reason.** The shape when that happens: `ON ("org_id", "uploaded_at") WHERE "linked_to" is null`, org first since it is the equality predicate and `uploaded_at` supplies the ordering and the range. ⚠️ **Re-measure before assuming the rewrite helps** — with a small table and a narrow partial index the planner may reasonably prefer the existing one either way, and an index changed on reasoning alone is a second recorded assumption nobody checked. ⛔ **Revisit at the latest when a real organisation's `stored_files` passes a few thousand unlinked rows**, which is also roughly when the `.limit(500)` cap DEF-306 describes starts mattering — the two want looking at together |

| 2026-09-06 | **T-225 built** — the half day that was nearly a full day. Ian's call, arising from `lipa-0359`: someone who worked **8 hours** against a 9-hour requirement was credited a **5-hour half day** with 3h pending, and nothing told anybody to look at it. ⭐ **The measurement changed the design, and that is the transferable part.** Ian proposed flagging days like this; flagging *every* short day would have fired on **64 of 346 sessions (18.5%)** — a queue nobody reads, and **a rubber-stamped queue is worse than a rule because it launders a guess as a decision.** The corpus showed why: **45 of those 64 worked only 50–59% of the requirement**, so the collapse is correct for the overwhelming majority and the problem is a thin tail. The rule ships as *"worked more than the midpoint between the half day and the requirement"* — **derived from each store's own two numbers**, so 7h at a 9h store and 7.5h at a 10h one, with no constant to drift or to wonder about. It fires on **8 of 346**, every one at **80–90%** of a full day. ⛔ **Not one credited minute moves**, asserted over the whole corpus rather than claimed. ⚠️ **A second, pre-existing defect was found while surfacing it**: `/attendance/[dayId]` told managers *"a day with an unresolved anomaly cannot be verified"*, which was **already untrue** — `BLOCKING_ANOMALIES` is an explicit allowlist and `start_not_allowed` is deliberately advisory (blocking it would hold a store's pay for DEF-087's stale grid). Prose claiming what the code does not do, the shape this project has now filed ten defects about. The screen renders ⛔ against blocking items and ⚠️ *"advisory, does not block sign-off"* against the rest, and the blocking sentence appears only when something actually blocks. ⚠️ **Open for Ian and deliberately NOT defaulted**: what a "permitted undertime" override should PAY. Recommendation is the time actually worked (8h), not the full requirement — v1 paid the full shift and **over-paid relative to presence in 58 of 346 sessions**, so inheriting that would be inheriting a defect rather than choosing a policy. |
| 2026-09-06 | **T-214 built — Phase 6 closes.** The responsiveness scorecard, and the clock findings do not have. ⭐ **Specifying it established it could not be ported at all**: v1's score needs a finding's FIRST-SEEN date, and a v2 finding is a pure function of current state that carries no timestamp — so two thirds of the task was building `finding_occurrences`, an append-only observation log written by a new daily scheduler job. ⛔ **Nothing in the detection path reads it**, asserted by deleting every row and re-reading the feed, so bible row 123 stands. ⭐ **The design finding: the naive port re-opens the hole T-135 closed** — mapping v1's *resolved* onto v2's *acknowledged* pays managers to ACCEPT risk, so the score counts occurrences that CLEARED and acknowledgement is reported but never scored. ⚠️ **Three self-inflicted errors worth recording, because none was caught by a test.** (1) The first render scored three innocent branches **0** — their findings had been raised that morning, and `cleared / raised` cannot distinguish "ignored" from "not yet had a chance"; findings inside a 14-day grace window are now *pending*, and **every test passed both before and after the fix**. (2) Four of the new tests were green only because the table happened to be empty — the count queries were not scoped by `org_id`, and the second run read `expected '8' to be '2'`. (3) ⛔ **A wrong claim reached a pushed document**: the migration-`0129` sweep in that morning's handoff said "3 of 9 databases, `shareat_test` missing". It is **4 of 10 and `shareat_test` has it** — the probe built each URL with a string `replace`, and this project's Postgres password contains an `@`, so it connected somewhere else and reported confident nonsense. **The tell was there and was not read: a database supposedly missing a migration whose own suite was green.** Use `new URL()` and set `.pathname`; never split a connection string by hand. ⚠️ `migrate.ts` already carries a comment about that `@` (DEF-030) — the knowledge existed and was not consulted. (4) ⭐ **The sharpest one, and the full suite caught it where the targeted run could not.** Migration `0130` was applied to four databases **by hand**, which runs the SQL but does **not** write Drizzle's `drizzle.__drizzle_migrations` journal. The databases were correct; the journal was not — so the real migrator considered `0130` unapplied and `journal.test.ts`'s *"re-running the migration is a no-op"* failed with `relation "finding_occurrences" already exists`. ⛔ **A hand-applied migration is not an applied migration**, and the divergence is invisible to every test that does not run the migrator itself — DEF-039/DEF-094's family. Fixed by reverting the hand-applied objects on all four and letting `migrate()` apply and record them (journal 113 → 114 on each). ⚠️ `shareat_dev` additionally refused the revert because two real `finding_observation` rows already existed in `scheduler_runs` from manual job runs — a constraint doing its job. |
